- **fsa-ch01-introduction**: Frames software architecture as four interlocking dimensions (characteristics, logical components, style, decisions) analyzed in that order, applies three laws (trade-offs everywhere, rationale over mechanism, decisions on a spectrum) and the eight expectations of an architect. Based on Chapter 1 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when scoping an architecture effort, separating architectural from technical decisions, challenging "no trade-off" claims, checking decision compliance or vitality, or coaching someone growing into the architect role. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch01-introduction))
- **fsa-ch02-architectural-thinking**: Applies the architectural-thinking mindset from Chapter 2 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Covers placing a decision on the architecture-versus-design spectrum, two-sided trade-off analysis, translating business drivers into architecture characteristics, building technical breadth (knowledge pyramid, daily learning habit, personal technology radar), and staying hands-on without becoming a bottleneck. Use when deciding who owns a decision, comparing options, planning an architect's learning, or advising a developer moving into architecture. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch02-architectural-thinking))
- **fsa-ch03-modularity**: Modularity analysis for architects, based on Chapter 3 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Covers modularity versus granularity, the cohesion scale, LCOM, afferent/efferent coupling, abstractness, instability, distance from the main sequence (zones of pain and uselessness) and connascence (forms, strength, locality, degree). Use when sizing or splitting modules or services, reviewing coupling, assessing a code base before a migration, measuring technical debt, or naming a coupling problem in a code or design review. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch03-modularity))
- **fsa-ch04-characteristics-defined**: Defines what counts as an architecture characteristic, how to tell it apart from domain requirements and ordinary design hygiene, how to categorize and name characteristics (operational, structural, cloud, cross-cutting, ISO families), and why to keep the set small and accept least-worst trade-offs. Based on Chapter 4 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when sorting a requirements list into domain vs. capabilities, deciding whether a concern needs structural support, building a shared glossary of "-ilities", or pruning an overlong list of quality goal… ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch04-characteristics-defined))
- **fsa-ch05-identifying-characteristics**: Guides identification of architecture characteristics ("-ilities") from domain concerns, requirements and implicit domain knowledge; decomposing composite goals like agility; telling scalability from elasticity; deciding architecture versus design; and pruning to a short prioritized list via a worksheet and top-three selection. Based on Chapter 5 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when gathering non-functional requirements, translating business goals into -ilities, running an architecture kata, or running a stakeholder prioritization session. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch05-identifying-characteristics))
- **fsa-ch06-measuring-and-governing**: Turns vague architecture characteristics into objective operational, structural and process measures, and governs them with automated fitness functions (cycle checks, layer rules, main-sequence distance, complexity gates, chaos-style production checks). Based on Chapter 6 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when defining measurable "-ilities", choosing latency or deployability metrics, setting Cyclomatic Complexity thresholds, writing architecture tests for CI, or stopping modularity erosion. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch06-measuring-and-governing))
- **fsa-ch07-scope-of-characteristics**: Scopes architecture characteristics with the architecture quantum (independently deployable, cohesive unit with its data) and uses that scope to pick monolithic vs distributed styles, draw service boundaries, choose persistence and sync vs async communication, and scope cloud-hosted parts. Based on Chapter 7 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed). Use when deciding whether one set of characteristics fits the whole system, finding quanta, spotting shared-database or shared-library coupling, or diagnosing failing calls between services with mismatched scalabilit… ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch07-scope-of-characteristics))
- **fsa-ch08-component-based-thinking**: Guides identifying, sizing and refining logical components and analyzing coupling between them, based on Chapter 8 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when drawing a logical architecture before a physical one, seeding components with the Workflow or Actor/Action approach, spotting Entity Trap "Manager" components, assigning user stories, splitting components for cohesion or architecture characteristics, computing afferent/efferent coupling, finding temporal coupling, or applying the Law of Demeter. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch08-component-based-thinking))
- **fsa-ch09-style-foundations**: Foundational decision rules for architecture styles, based on Chapter 9 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Covers style versus pattern, Big Ball of Mud, client/server and three-tier lineage, technical versus domain top-level partitioning, Conway's Law, monolithic versus distributed, the eleven fallacies of distributed computing, and team topologies. Use when choosing monolith versus distributed, deciding how to partition components, reviewing a plan to split a system into services, or estimating latency, availability and bandwidth of remote call chains. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch09-style-foundations))
- **fsa-ch10-layered-style**: Guidance for evaluating, designing and governing the layered (n-tier) architecture style, based on Chapter 10 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Covers logical and physical layer topology, open vs closed layers, layers of isolation, the Architecture Sinkhole 80/20 check, cloud, risk, governance and team fit. Use when choosing or rejecting a layered monolith, reviewing layer dependencies, deciding which layers may be skipped, writing layer fitness functions, or diagnosing a layered system that is outgrowing itself. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch10-layered-style))
- **fsa-ch11-modular-monolith**: Guidance for evaluating, designing and governing a modular monolith (one deployable, code partitioned by business domain), based on Chapter 11 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Covers single-repo vs per-module artifacts, peer-to-peer vs mediator communication, data topology, risks, fitness functions and team alignment. Use when choosing a starting architecture, restructuring a layered monolith by domain, reviewing module boundaries, writing boundary checks, or deciding when a monolith has outgrown itself. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch11-modular-monolith))
- **fsa-ch12-pipeline-style**: Guidance for evaluating, designing and governing the pipeline (pipes and filters) architecture style, based on Chapter 12 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Covers filter roles (producer, transformer, tester, consumer), pipe contracts, data and cloud topologies, risks, tag-based governance, team fit and trade-offs. Use when a user designs ETL, EDI, telemetry or step-by-step workflows, asks whether pipes and filters fits, reviews an overloaded or bidirectional pipeline, or weighs monolithic versus distributed filters. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch12-pipeline-style))
- **fsa-ch13-microkernel-style**: Guides evaluation, design and governance of the microkernel (plug-in) architecture style: stable core vs volatile plug-ins, registries, contracts, data ownership, remote plug-ins, cloud placement, risks, fitness functions and team split. Based on Chapter 13 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when a system needs per-client, per-jurisdiction or per-product customization, when deciding whether a plug-in design is appropriate, when designing a core/plug-in boundary, or when reviewing an existing plug-in system for drift. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch13-microkernel-style))
- **fsa-ch14-service-based-style**: Applies the service-based architecture style (coarse-grained domain services, optional shared database) from Chapter 14 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when evaluating whether a system should be service-based versus monolithic or microservices, designing domain services, UIs, gateways or database topology, managing shared-database schema change, governing service count and coupling, aligning teams, or planning a stepping-stone migration toward microservices. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch14-service-based-style))
- **fsa-ch15-event-driven-style**: Guidance on the event-driven architecture style based on Chapter 15 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Covers events vs messages, derived and extensible events, payload design (data-based vs key-based, anemic events, Swarm of Gnats), error handling, data-loss prevention, request-reply, mediator vs choreography, data topologies, risks, governance and team fit. Use when deciding whether EDA fits, designing or reviewing event flows and contracts, choosing a mediator or database topology, or writing fitness functions for an event-driven system. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch15-event-driven-style))
- **fsa-ch16-space-based-style**: Guidance for evaluating, designing and governing space-based architectures (processing units, replicated in-memory data grids, data pumps, readers and writers, elastic deployment), based on Chapter 16 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture". Use when a system faces extreme or spiky concurrency, when choosing replicated versus distributed caching, estimating data-collision risk, planning cache-to-database sync, defining fitness functions for memory and sync time, aligning teams, or deciding whether the style is worth its cost and complexity. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch16-space-based-style))
- **fsa-ch17-orchestration-driven-soa**: Guidance on orchestration-driven service-oriented architecture (SOA) and its modern legacy, based on Chapter 17 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture". Covers the layered service taxonomy, orchestration engines and ESBs, reuse-versus-coupling trade-offs, canonical entity pitfalls, single-quantum characteristics, the Accidental SOA antipattern, and fitness functions for integration hubs. Use when evaluating an ESB or central workflow engine, reviewing shared "canonical" services, diagnosing slow cross-layer change, or designing legacy-to-modern integration. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch17-orchestration-driven-soa))
- **fsa-ch18-microservices**: Applies the microservices architecture style as described in Chapter 18 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Covers bounded-context boundaries, service granularity, data isolation and database topologies, API layer limits, sidecars and service mesh, choreography vs orchestration, sagas, governance of coupling, team topologies, and fit. Use when deciding whether microservices suits a system, sizing or splitting services, choosing a data topology, reviewing a microservices design for risks, or setting up coupling governance. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch18-microservices))
- **fsa-ch19-choosing-a-style**: Guides selection of an architecture style (monolith vs distributed, layered, modular monolith, microkernel, microservices, event-driven, space-based, service-based) from domain knowledge, architecture characteristics, data, cloud, organizational and team factors, and domain/architecture shape fit. Based on Chapter 19 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.). Use when asked which architecture style to pick, whether to split a system, where data should live, sync vs async between services, or how to judge an industry architecture trend. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch19-choosing-a-style))
- **fsa-ch20-architectural-patterns**: Applies Chapter 20 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.) on architectural patterns as contextual solutions distinct from styles, best practices and tools. Covers separating domain from operational coupling (Hexagonal/Ports and Adapters, Sidecar, Service Mesh, orthogonal reuse), orchestration vs choreography, CQRS, and Single-Broker vs Domain-Broker infrastructure. Use when choosing or naming a pattern, standardizing cross-cutting concerns across services, designing workflow coordination, splitting reads from writes, or deciding broker topology in event-drive… ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch20-architectural-patterns))
- **fsa-ch21-architectural-decisions**: Applies Chapter 21 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.) on making, justifying, recording and communicating architectural decisions. Covers the Covering Your Assets, Groundhog Day and Email-Driven Architecture antipatterns, the last responsible moment, architectural significance, and Architectural Decision Records (sections, status and RFC, approval criteria, compliance, storage, standards, legacy systems, AI use). Use when deciding whether or when to decide, writing or reviewing an ADR, setting approval rules, or asking an LLM for architecture advice. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch21-architectural-decisions))
- **fsa-ch22-analyzing-risk**: Applies Chapter 22 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.) on analyzing architecture risk. Covers the impact x likelihood risk matrix, risk assessments by characteristic and domain with totals, filtering and trend direction, three-phase risk storming (identification, consensus, mitigation), unknown-technology scoring, cost-versus-risk negotiation, user-story risk and mitigation patterns. Use when scoring a risk, building a risk report, facilitating or aggregating a risk-storming session, or proposing mitigations. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch22-analyzing-risk))
- **fsa-ch23-diagramming**: Applies Chapter 23 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.) on diagramming architecture. Covers representational consistency across views, the Irrational Artifact Attachment antipattern, low- versus high-fidelity artifacts, tool features and semantic layers, UML, C4 and ArchiMate, and guidelines for titles, lines, shapes, labels, color and keys. Use when drawing, generating (Mermaid, PlantUML, Structurizr) or reviewing an architecture diagram, choosing a notation standard, or planning how to present an architecture to stakeholders. - **fsa-ch24-making-teams-effective**: Applies Chapter 24 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.) on how architects make development teams effective: bidirectional collaboration, constraints as a right-sized "room", control-freak vs armchair vs effective architects, five-factor elastic leadership scoring, team-size warning signs (process loss, pluralistic ignorance, diffusion of responsibility), checklists, and library decision rights. Use when calibrating architect involvement, diagnosing team dysfunction, drafting team checklists, or setting who decides on libraries and frameworks. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch24-making-teams-effective))
- **fsa-ch25-negotiation-and-leadership**: Applies Chapter 25 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.) on negotiation and leadership. Covers negotiating with business stakeholders, peer architects and developers, the 4 Cs, essential vs accidental complexity, pragmatic yet visionary design, leading by example, and controlling meetings to stay with the team. Use when preparing for a contested decision, pushing back on an extreme requirement (e.g. five nines), resolving a disagreement, phrasing a request to developers, or auditing an architect's meeting load. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch25-negotiation-and-leadership))
- **fsa-ch26-architectural-intersections**: Applies Chapter 26 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.) on architectural intersections, the nine facets an architecture must align with: implementation (operational concerns, structure, constraints), infrastructure, data topologies, engineering practices, team topologies, systems integration, enterprise standards, business environment, and generative AI. Use when validating a design, diagnosing why a sound style fails in production, choosing a database type or topology, checking team or pipeline fit, or adding LLMs to a system. - **fsa-ch27-laws-revisited**: Applies Chapter 27 of Richards and Ford's "Fundamentals of Software Architecture" (2nd ed.), which revisits the three laws of software architecture: trade-offs are unavoidable, rationale outranks mechanism, and decisions sit on spectrums rather than binaries. Covers contextual trade-off matrices, weighting, hidden trade-offs (reuse vs volatility, reuse vs decoupling), and the Out of Context antipattern. ([sauloleite/booksasskills](https://github.com/sauloleite/booksasskills/tree/HEAD/fundamentals-of-software-architecture-richards-ford/fsa-ch27-laws-revisited))
